========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Thank you. I, um... I guess I'm here because I just keep blowing up at people over absolutely nothing. And I mean nothing at all. It’s getting to the point where I don't even recognize myself anymore. 

Like, just the other day at work—I work in retail, and I've been doing this a long time, so I know how things are supposed to run and how stressful it can get—but one of my employees, she just put some of the new stock on the wrong shelf. It was such a simple mistake, you know? Something that takes maybe two seconds to fix. Normally, I'd just gently point it out, maybe show her the right display section, and we'd move on. But instead, I just... I completely lost it. I yelled at her right there on the sales floor. I was practically screaming about it, and the poor girl looked absolutely terrified of me. 

And the worst part is, right after I did it, I had to go hide in the back stockroom because I was literally shaking. My whole body was just trembling so badly I couldn't even hold a pen. Because that is not how I usually treat people. I am never like that. I'm usually the patient one, the one who helps everyone else keep calm, but lately, it's like this awful switch flips inside me and I just explode. It's exhausting, and I feel terrible about it, but I just can't seem to stop it from happening.

Yes, exactly, that's exactly what I mean. Like I was just saying with that poor girl and the stock on the wrong shelf... my reaction was way, way bigger than the situation called for. I mean, putting a box of items on the middle shelf instead of the bottom shelf is not a crisis, you know? It's literally nothing. 

And I wasn't even having a bad day before that happened! I had my coffee, the store wasn't even busy yet, it was a Tuesday morning for heaven's sake, which is usually our slowest day of the whole week. So there was absolutely no reason for me to be stressed out. I was just walking the floor, doing my usual morning checks, and then I saw it and... boom. I just completely blew up at her. It was like going from zero to a hundred in a split second over something so trivial. 

And like I mentioned a minute ago, the aftermath of it... having to hide in the back room and just shaking like a leaf... it just proves to me how out of proportion the whole thing was. Because if I was just normally annoyed about a minor mistake, I wouldn't have a physical reaction like that, you know? I wouldn't be trembling so hard I couldn't even hold my inventory clipboard. It's just so unlike me, it really, really is. I've been managing people in retail for years, and you have to have a lot of patience for this industry. Which I always have had! I'm the calm one. But now, it doesn't matter if it's a slow day or if I'm completely fine one minute, the tiniest little thing sets me off and I'm yelling before I even realize what I'm doing.

No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down or hopeless. Well, that's the thing, it's not a constant, simmering kind of thing. I mean, I'm not walking around all day just gritting my teeth or feeling like everyone is annoying me. That's why it's so shocking when it happens! Like I was saying before, about yelling at that poor girl over the stock on the wrong shelf... I wasn't feeling irritable before that moment. And then I'm hiding in the back room shaking because, like I said, that is just not how I usually treat people. It's the suddenness of it, the way it just spikes out of nowhere, that's what's so terrifying. If I was just in a bad mood all day, at least I could warn people, you know? Like, 'Hey, I'm feeling cranky today, give me some space.' But I don't get that warning. I'm fine, I'm fine, I'm fine, and then I'm screaming.

No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I'm a good sleeper, usually. I need my solid eight hours, or I'm just dragging the next day, especially with being on my feet all day at the store. If my thoughts were racing and I was getting easily distracted, I wouldn't be able to do my job at all. I have a whole system with my clipboard and my color-coded pens—blue for scheduling, red for inventory issues, green for vendor notes—and I stick to it religiously. I've never had a period where I just couldn't focus or felt like my brain was going a million miles a minute. It's really just... it's just these sudden anger explosions. That's the only thing that's out of the ordinary for me. Everything else, my sleep, my focus, my energy levels, it's all just... normal. I do a lot of gardening, mostly container gardening on my patio because I don't have a huge yard, but I've got these gorgeous heirloom tomatoes growing right now, and I still go out there every evening to check on them and water them. I haven't lost interest in any of that. It's just... it's just this anger, this explosive anger that comes out of nowhere. That's the only thing that's really changed. I'm not sitting around crying or feeling like life is pointless, I'm just... I'm just terrified of when the next outburst is going to happen, you know? Well, yes, actually. That's... that's kind of the elephant in the room, I guess. My husband and I, we just bought this older house, a real fixer-upper, about a month ago. We were so excited to finally get out of our apartment and have some space, you know? The plan was to do a lot of the work ourselves on the weekends to save money. We had it all mapped out, room by room.

But then, during our very first weekend working on the house—this was about two weeks ago, exactly two weeks ago this past Saturday—we were starting on the kitchen. We were just trying to remove some of the old cabinets, and my husband was using this heat gun to soften some old adhesive, and... I don't even know how it happened so fast, but it caught the wall on fire. It was just this tiny spark at first, and then suddenly the whole corner behind the stove was up in flames. 

It was terrifying. The smoke was so thick and black, and it smelled awful, like melting plastic and old grease. I remember screaming at him to get out, and we were running around trying to find the fire extinguisher we'd literally just bought the day before, but in the panic, I couldn't remember where we put it. We ended up having to call 911 and wait outside on the lawn while the fire department came. They put it out quickly, thank God, and the structural damage wasn't too bad, mostly just smoke and water damage in that one room, but... 

I mean, ever since then, for the last two weeks, I just haven't been right. That's when all this started. These anger outbursts, the shaking, all of it. It's like a switch flipped the day of that fire, and I haven't been able to turn it back off. Every time I smell anything even remotely like smoke, even just someone grilling outside, my heart starts pounding. But yeah, that fire two weeks ago... that's definitely the only major thing that's happened.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't get those sudden panic attacks where I feel like I'm dying or losing control or anything. It's not like that. Like I said, the main thing is just the anger, the snapping at people. And the shaking afterwards, but that's just because I feel so awful about yelling. 

I mean, yes, if I smell smoke, like I mentioned, my heart will start pounding a little bit, and I get this rush of anxiety, but it's not a full-blown panic attack. It's just a reaction to the smell, you know? It makes sense. It reminds me of the fire. But it's not this out-of-the-blue, overwhelming terror where I can't breathe. I'm not hyperventilating or feeling like the walls are closing in. 

I've had friends who have panic attacks, and they describe it as this sudden, terrifying feeling that comes out of nowhere, where they literally think they're having a heart attack and need to go to the hospital. My friend Sarah, she used to get them all the time when we were in our twenties, and she would have to pull her car over on the side of the highway because she couldn't catch her breath. I've never had anything like that. Even when the fire was actually happening, I was panicked, of course, but it was a very specific, logical panic because my house was on fire! I was yelling and running around looking for the extinguisher, but I wasn't frozen in fear or feeling like I was losing my mind. 

So, no, since the fire, I haven't had any of those sudden, intense panic episodes you're talking about. I'm just dealing with the anger outbursts and the lingering anxiety when I smell something burning, but not the racing heart and shortness of breath out of nowhere. I'm pretty grounded, physically, I guess, except for the shaking after I get mad. But that's totally different, right? That's just adrenaline from the yelling, I think. Not a panic attack.

I'm fine, I'm fine, I'm fine, and then I'm screaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually energized, overly confident, or needed less sleep than usual, where your thoughts were racing or you were more easily distracted?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I'm a good sleeper, usually. I need my solid eight hours, or I'm just dragging the next day, especially with being on my feet all day at the store. I've never been one of those people who can just run on four hours of sleep and a pot of coffee. And as for feeling unusually energized or overly confident... definitely not. I mean, I know my job, I'm confident in my abilities as a manager because, like I said, I've been doing this for years, but I don't get these bursts of feeling like I can conquer the world or anything. \n\nMy thoughts don't really race, either. I'm pretty organized, I have to be to keep track of all the inventory and the schedules and the vendor deliveries. If my thoughts were racing and I was getting easily distracted, I wouldn't be able to do my job at all. I have a whole system with my clipboard and my color-coded pens—blue for scheduling, red for inventory issues, green for vendor notes—and I stick to it religiously. I've never had a period where I just couldn't focus or felt like my brain was going a million miles a minute. It's really just... it's just these sudden anger explosions. That's the only thing that's out of the ordinary for me. Everything else, my sleep, my focus, my energy levels, it's all just... normal. Well, except for the shaking after I yell at someone, but that's just because I'm so upset with myself, you know? But no, no racing thoughts or needing less sleep. Definitely not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or been through any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past that might still be affecting you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's... that's kind of the elephant in the room, I guess. My husband and I, we just bought this older house, a real fixer-upper, about a month ago. We were so excited to finally get out of our apartment and have some space, you know? The plan was to do a lot of the work ourselves on the weekends to save money. We had it all mapped out, room by room.\n\nBut then, during our very first weekend working on the house—this was about two weeks ago, exactly two weeks ago this past Saturday—we were starting on the kitchen. We were just trying to remove some of the old cabinets, and my husband was using this heat gun to soften some old adhesive, and... I don't even know how it happened so fast, but it caught the wall on fire. It was just this tiny spark at first, and then suddenly the whole corner behind the stove was up in flames. \n\nIt was terrifying. The smoke was so thick and black, and it smelled awful, like melting plastic and old grease. I remember screaming at him to get out, and we were running around trying to find the fire extinguisher we'd literally just bought the day before, but in the panic, I couldn't remember where we put it. Every time I smell anything even remotely like smoke, even just someone grilling outside, my heart starts pounding. I mean, I don't get those sudden panic attacks where I feel like I'm dying or losing control or anything. It's not like that. Like I said, the main thing is just the anger, the snapping at people. It's just a reaction to the smell, you know? It makes sense. It reminds me of the fire. But it's not this out-of-the-blue, overwhelming terror where I can't breathe. I'm not hyperventilating or feeling like the walls are closing in. \n\nI've had friends who have panic attacks, and they describe it as this sudden, terrifying feeling that comes out of nowhere, where they literally think they're having a heart attack and need to go to the hospital. Yes. Oh my gosh, yes. It's awful, actually. It's like my brain just won't let it go, no matter how hard I try to focus on other things, and I really do try. It’s not even like I’m sitting there dwelling on it on purpose, you know? It just... the fire keeps jumping into my head completely out of nowhere. 

I could be doing the most mundane, everyday things. Like, I'll be at work, just sitting at my desk in the back office, opening up a completely normal inventory spreadsheet on the computer—something I do literally a dozen times a day to check the stock levels—and suddenly, bam, I'm right back there. Or I'll be at home, or well, at my mother-in-law's since we can't really cook in our place right now, and I'll just pick up a frying pan to make eggs or something, and it's like I'm instantly transported back to that Saturday. 

It's so vivid, too. It's not just a passing thought. I can literally smell that awful, toxic smoke all over again, that melting plastic smell I was telling you about earlier. And I can actually see the kitchen just filling up with this thick, black smoke, right in front of my eyes, before I can even stop the memory from happening. It just takes over. 

And it's so frustrating because I'll be right in the middle of looking at my spreadsheet or talking to one of my cashiers, and I have to grip the edge of the desk or the handle of the pan because the memory is just so intense and real. I try to shake my head and clear it out, I really do, but once it starts, it just forces its way in. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to look at my spreadsheets and do my job without feeling like my kitchen is burning down all over again. I mean, it's just a pan, you know? But my brain doesn't seem to care. It just flashes right back to the smoke.

It’s getting to the point where I don't even recognize myself anymore. \n\nLike, just the other day at work—I work in retail, and I've been doing this a long time, so I know how things are supposed to run and how stressful it can get—but one of my employees, she just put some of the new stock on the wrong shelf. It was such a simple mistake, you know? Something that takes maybe two seconds to fix. Normally, I'd just gently point it out, maybe show her the right display section, and we'd move on. It's exhausting, and I feel terrible about it, but I just can't seem to stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these outbursts happen even when you're not particularly stressed or if they seem way bigger than the situation calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly, that's exactly what I mean. Like I was just saying with that poor girl and the stock on the wrong shelf... my reaction was way, way bigger than the situation called for. I mean, putting a box of items on the middle shelf instead of the bottom shelf is not a crisis, you know? It's literally nothing. \n\nAnd I wasn't even having a bad day before that happened! I had my coffee, the store wasn't even busy yet, it was a Tuesday morning for heaven's sake, which is usually our slowest day of the whole week. So there was absolutely no reason for me to be stressed out. I was just walking the floor, doing my usual morning checks, and then I saw it and... boom. I just completely blew up at her. It was like going from zero to a hundred in a split second over something so trivial. \n\nAnd like I mentioned a minute ago, the aftermath of it... having to hide in the back room and just shaking like a leaf... it just proves to me how out of proportion the whole thing was. Because if I was just normally annoyed about a minor mistake, I wouldn't have a physical reaction like that, you know? I wouldn't be trembling so hard I couldn't even hold my inventory clipboard. It's just so unlike me, it really, really is. I've been managing people in retail for years, and you have to have a lot of patience for this industry. Which I always have had! I'm the calm one. But now, it doesn't matter if it's a slow day or if I'm completely fine one minute, the tiniest little thing sets me off and I'm yelling before I even realize what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any episodes of feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, separate from these anger outbursts?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down or hopeless. I mean, I'm upset about the anger, obviously, because like I said, it's just so unlike me, but it's not like a deep sadness or depression or anything like that. I still look forward to things. Like, my sister and I, we have this tradition where we go to the farmer's market every Sunday morning—rain or shine, we're always there by 8 AM to get the best produce and these amazing scones from this one specific bakery stand—and I still love doing that. I still get excited about it. \n\nAnd I still like my hobbies, too. I do a lot of gardening, mostly container gardening on my patio because I don't have a huge yard, but I've got these gorgeous heirloom tomatoes growing right now, and I still go out there every evening to check on them and water them. I haven't lost interest in any of that. It's just... it's just this anger, this explosive anger that comes out of nowhere. That's the only thing that's really changed. I'm not sitting around crying or feeling like life is pointless, I'm just... I'm just terrified of when the next outburst is going to happen, you know? But as far as feeling hopeless or losing interest in my life, no, that hasn't been happening at all. I still want to do all my normal things, I just want to do them without feeling like a ticking time bomb.\n\nDoctor: Between these outbursts, do you find yourself feeling constantly irritable or on edge, even when nothing is really wrong?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, it's not a constant, simmering kind of thing. I mean, I'm not walking around all day just gritting my teeth or feeling like everyone is annoying me. Between the outbursts, I feel pretty much like my normal self, honestly. I'm just going about my day, doing my job, talking to customers, checking inventory... I'm not on edge in the sense that I'm waiting for a fight. \n\nThat's why it's so shocking when it happens! Like I was saying before, about yelling at that poor girl over the stock on the wrong shelf... I wasn't feeling irritable before that moment. I wasn't pacing around feeling tense. I was perfectly fine. And then, bam, I'm blowing up at people over absolutely nothing. And it really is nothing! And then I'm hiding in the back room shaking because, like I said, that is just not how I usually treat people. It's the suddenness of it, the way it just spikes out of nowhere, that's what's so terrifying. If I was just in a bad mood all day, at least I could warn people, you know? Like, 'Hey, I'm feeling cranky today, give me some space.' But I don't get that warning. I'm fine, I'm fine, I'm fine, and then I'm screaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually energized, overly confident, or needed less sleep than usual, where your thoughts were racing or you were more easily distracted?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I'm a good sleeper, usually. I need my solid eight hours, or I'm just dragging the next day, especially with being on my feet all day at the store. I've never been one of those people who can just run on four hours of sleep and a pot of coffee. And as for feeling unusually energized or overly confident... definitely not. I mean, I know my job, I'm confident in my abilities as a manager because, like I said, I've been doing this for years, but I don't get these bursts of feeling like I can conquer the world or anything. \n\nMy thoughts don't really race, either. I'm pretty organized, I have to be to keep track of all the inventory and the schedules and the vendor deliveries. If my thoughts were racing and I was getting easily distracted, I wouldn't be able to do my job at all. I have a whole system with my clipboard and my color-coded pens—blue for scheduling, red for inventory issues, green for vendor notes—and I stick to it religiously. I've never had a period where I just couldn't focus or felt like my brain was going a million miles a minute. It's really just... it's just these sudden anger explosions. That's the only thing that's out of the ordinary for me. Everything else, my sleep, my focus, my energy levels, it's all just... normal. Well, except for the shaking after I yell at someone, but that's just because I'm so upset with myself, you know? But no, no racing thoughts or needing less sleep. Definitely not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or been through any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past that might still be affecting you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's... that's kind of the elephant in the room, I guess. My husband and I, we just bought this older house, a real fixer-upper, about a month ago. We were so excited to finally get out of our apartment and have some space, you know? The plan was to do a lot of the work ourselves on the weekends to save money. We had it all mapped out, room by room.\n\nBut then, during our very first weekend working on the house—this was about two weeks ago, exactly two weeks ago this past Saturday—we were starting on the kitchen. We were just trying to remove some of the old cabinets, and my husband was using this heat gun to soften some old adhesive, and... I don't even know how it happened so fast, but it caught the wall on fire. It was just this tiny spark at first, and then suddenly the whole corner behind the stove was up in flames. \n\nIt was terrifying. The smoke was so thick and black, and it smelled awful, like melting plastic and old grease. I remember screaming at him to get out, and we were running around trying to find the fire extinguisher we'd literally just bought the day before, but in the panic, I couldn't remember where we put it. We ended up having to call 911 and wait outside on the lawn while the fire department came. They put it out quickly, thank God, and the structural damage wasn't too bad, mostly just smoke and water damage in that one room, but... \n\nI mean, ever since then, for the last two weeks, I just haven't been right. That's when all this started. These anger outbursts, the shaking, all of it. It's like a switch flipped the day of that fire, and I haven't been able to turn it back off. Every time I smell anything even remotely like smoke, even just someone grilling outside, my heart starts pounding. But yeah, that fire two weeks ago... that's definitely the only major thing that's happened.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear, like you were having a panic attack, maybe with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you were losing control?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't get those sudden panic attacks where I feel like I'm dying or losing control or anything. It's not like that. Like I said, the main thing is just the anger, the snapping at people. And the shaking afterwards, but that's just because I feel so awful about yelling. \n\nI mean, yes, if I smell smoke, like I mentioned, my heart will start pounding a little bit, and I get this rush of anxiety, but it's not a full-blown panic attack. It's just a reaction to the smell, you know? It makes sense. It reminds me of the fire. But it's not this out-of-the-blue, overwhelming terror where I can't breathe. I'm not hyperventilating or feeling like the walls are closing in. \n\nI've had friends who have panic attacks, and they describe it as this sudden, terrifying feeling that comes out of nowhere, where they literally think they're having a heart attack and need to go to the hospital. My friend Sarah, she used to get them all the time when we were in our twenties, and she would have to pull her car over on the side of the highway because she couldn't catch her breath. I've never had anything like that. Even when the fire was actually happening, I was panicked, of course, but it was a very specific, logical panic because my house was on fire! I was yelling and running around looking for the extinguisher, but I wasn't frozen in fear or feeling like I was losing my mind. \n\nSo, no, since the fire, I haven't had any of those sudden, intense panic episodes you're talking about. I'm just dealing with the anger outbursts and the lingering anxiety when I smell something burning, but not the racing heart and shortness of breath out of nowhere. I'm pretty grounded, physically, I guess, except for the shaking after I get mad. But that's totally different, right? That's just adrenaline from the yelling, I think. Not a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you found yourself having upsetting thoughts or memories about it that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not even like I’m sitting there dwelling on it on purpose, you know? It just... the fire keeps jumping into my head completely out of nowhere. \n\nI could be doing the most mundane, everyday things. Or I'll be at home, or well, at my mother-in-law's since we can't really cook in our place right now, and I'll just pick up a frying pan to make eggs or something, and it's like I'm instantly transported back to that Saturday. \n\nIt's so vivid, too. It's not just a passing thought. I can literally smell that awful, toxic smoke all over again, that melting plastic smell I was telling you about earlier. It just takes over. \n\nAnd it's so frustrating because I'll be right in the middle of looking at my spreadsheet or talking to one of my cashiers, and I have to grip the edge of the desk or the handle of the pan because the memory is just so intense and real. They put it out quickly, thank God, and the structural damage wasn't too bad, mostly just smoke and water damage in that one room, but... \n\nI mean, ever since then, for the last two weeks, I just haven't been right. That's when all this started. These anger outbursts, the shaking, all of it. It's like a switch flipped the day of that fire, and I haven't been able to turn it back off. Every time I smell anything even remotely like smoke, even just someone grilling outside, my heart starts pounding. But yeah, that fire two weeks ago... that's definitely the only major thing that's happened.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear, like you were having a panic attack, maybe with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you were losing control?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't get those sudden panic attacks where I feel like I'm dying or losing control or anything. It's not like that. Like I said, the main thing is just the anger, the snapping at people. And the shaking afterwards, but that's just because I feel so awful about yelling. \n\nI mean, yes, if I smell smoke, like I mentioned, my heart will start pounding a little bit, and I get this rush of anxiety, but it's not a full-blown panic attack. It's just a reaction to the smell, you know? It makes sense. It reminds me of the fire. But it's not this out-of-the-blue, overwhelming terror where I can't breathe. I'm not hyperventilating or feeling like the walls are closing in. \n\nI've had friends who have panic attacks, and they describe it as this sudden, terrifying feeling that comes out of nowhere, where they literally think they're having a heart attack and need to go to the hospital. My friend Sarah, she used to get them all the time when we were in our twenties, and she would have to pull her car over on the side of the highway because she couldn't catch her breath. I've never had anything like that. Even when the fire was actually happening, I was panicked, of course, but it was a very specific, logical panic because my house was on fire! I was yelling and running around looking for the extinguisher, but I wasn't frozen in fear or feeling like I was losing my mind. \n\nSo, no, since the fire, I haven't had any of those sudden, intense panic episodes you're talking about. I'm just dealing with the anger outbursts and the lingering anxiety when I smell something burning, but not the racing heart and shortness of breath out of nowhere. Oh my gosh, yes. It's awful, actually. It's like my brain just won't let it go, no matter how hard I try to focus on other things, and I really do try. It just... the fire keeps jumping into my head completely out of nowhere. \n\nI could be doing the most mundane, everyday things. Like, I'll be at work, just sitting at my desk in the back office, opening up a completely normal inventory spreadsheet on the computer—something I do literally a dozen times a day to check the stock levels—and suddenly, bam, I'm right back there. Or I'll be at home, or well, at my mother-in-law's since we can't really cook in our place right now, and I'll just pick up a frying pan to make eggs or something, and it's like I'm instantly transported back to that Saturday. \n\nIt's so vivid, too. It's not just a passing thought. I can literally smell that awful, toxic smoke all over again, that melting plastic smell I was telling you about earlier. It just takes over. \n\nAnd it's so frustrating because I'll be right in the middle of looking at my spreadsheet or talking to one of my cashiers, and I have to grip the edge of the desk or the handle of the pan because the memory is just so intense and real. I try to shake my head and clear it out, I really do, but once it starts, it just forces its way in. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to look at my spreadsheets and do my job without feeling like my kitchen is burning down all over again. I mean, it's just a pan, you know? But my brain doesn't seem to care. Oh, sleep? You know, you would think so, right? With everything else going on, those images popping up at work and the anger... you'd think my nights would just be a total disaster. My husband, actually, he was asking me the exact same thing just the other morning. He was like, "Are you having bad dreams?" because he knows how stressed I've been since that Saturday.

I think I'm just so completely exhausted from dealing with the memories during the day, and all that adrenaline from when I get angry and start shaking... by the time night rolls around, my body just completely shuts down. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in a cold sweat thinking about the smoke. I sleep right through until my alarm goes off at 6:15 for work. It's actually the only time I get any peace, to be perfectly honest with you. During the day, my brain is just going a mile a minute, but at night... nothing. Just blank, heavy sleep. 

I guess I should count myself lucky for that, at least, because if I wasn't sleeping on top of everything else, I don't know how I'd be functioning at the store at all. Managing the floor and dealing with customers is hard enough when you're fully rested, you know? So, no, no bad dreams or anything like that. Just... I'm just really tired by the time I get to bed.

I'm not hyperventilating or feeling like the walls are closing in. \n\nI've had friends who have panic attacks, and they describe it as this sudden, terrifying feeling that comes out of nowhere, where they literally think they're having a heart attack and need to go to the hospital. My friend Sarah, she used to get them all the time when we were in our twenties, and she would have to pull her car over on the side of the highway because she couldn't catch her breath. I've never had anything like that. Even when the fire was actually happening, I was panicked, of course, but it was a very specific, logical panic because my house was on fire! I was yelling and running around looking for the extinguisher, but I wasn't frozen in fear or feeling like I was losing my mind. \n\nSo, no, since the fire, I haven't had any of those sudden, intense panic episodes you're talking about. I'm just dealing with the anger outbursts and the lingering anxiety when I smell something burning, but not the racing heart and shortness of breath out of nowhere. I'm pretty grounded, physically, I guess, except for the shaking after I get mad. But that's totally different, right? That's just adrenaline from the yelling, I think. Not a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you found yourself having upsetting thoughts or memories about it that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes. Oh my gosh, yes. It's awful, actually. It's like my brain just won't let it go, no matter how hard I try to focus on other things, and I really do try. It’s not even like I’m sitting there dwelling on it on purpose, you know? It just... the fire keeps jumping into my head completely out of nowhere. \n\nI could be doing the most mundane, everyday things. Like, I'll be at work, just sitting at my desk in the back office, opening up a completely normal inventory spreadsheet on the computer—something I do literally a dozen times a day to check the stock levels—and suddenly, bam, I'm right back there. Or I'll be at home, or well, at my mother-in-law's since we can't really cook in our place right now, and I'll just pick up a frying pan to make eggs or something, and it's like I'm instantly transported back to that Saturday. \n\nIt's so vivid, too. It's not just a passing thought. I can literally smell that awful, toxic smoke all over again, that melting plastic smell I was telling you about earlier. And I can actually see the kitchen just filling up with this thick, black smoke, right in front of my eyes, before I can even stop the memory from happening. It just takes over. \n\nAnd it's so frustrating because I'll be right in the middle of looking at my spreadsheet or talking to one of my cashiers, and I have to grip the edge of the desk or the handle of the pan because the memory is just so intense and real. I try to shake my head and clear it out, I really do, but once it starts, it just forces its way in. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to look at my spreadsheets and do my job without feeling like my kitchen is burning down all over again. I mean, it's just a pan, you know? But my brain doesn't seem to care. It just flashes right back to the smoke.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you been having nightmares or trouble sleeping that might be related to what happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, sleep? You know, you would think so, right? With everything else going on, those images popping up at work and the anger... you'd think my nights would just be a total disaster. My husband, actually, he was asking me the exact same thing just the other morning. He was like, \"Are you having bad dreams?\" because he knows how stressed I've been since that Saturday. \n\nBut honestly? No. I really haven't had any nightmares about the fire. Not a single one. It’s strange, almost. I mean, I go to bed, usually around 10:30 or 11:00 after we watch a little TV in his mom's living room—we've been watching this baking show just to keep our minds off our own ruined kitchen, ironically enough—and once my head hits the pillow, I am just out cold. \n\nI think I'm just so completely exhausted from dealing with the memories during the day, and all that adrenaline from when I get angry and start shaking... by the time night rolls around, my body just completely shuts down. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in a cold sweat thinking about the smoke. I sleep right through until my alarm goes off at 6:15 for work. It's actually the only time I get any peace, to be perfectly honest with you. During the day, my brain is just going a mile a minute, but at night... nothing. Just blank, heavy sleep. \n\nI guess I should count myself lucky for that, at least, because if I wasn't sleeping on top of everything else, I don't know how I'd be functioning at the store at all. Managing the floor and dealing with customers is hard enough when you're fully rested, you know? So, no, no bad dreams or anything like that. Just... I'm just really tired by the time I get to bed.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince the fire, have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities that remind you of what happened?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / retail_salesperson / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been avoiding the house, the mortgage folder, and even the herbs on my patio because anything tied to that day makes my chest tighten and I just can’t make myself go near it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Avoid? Yes. Oh, absolutely. I mean, I’ve been completely avoiding the house itself, for starters. My husband goes over there to deal with the contractors and the insurance adjusters, and he keeps asking if I want to come with him, just to see the progress or help make decisions about the cleanup, but I just... I can't do it. I can't even drive down our street. Just the thought of pulling into that driveway makes my chest tighten up so much, like there’s this heavy, physical weight sitting right on my ribs. 

And it’s not just the physical house, either. It’s anything even remotely connected to it or that day. Like, the mortgage folder. My husband brought this thick green folder over to his mom's house—it has all our home insurance policies and the mortgage documents in it, because obviously we have to file all these claims and figure out how we're paying for everything. It’s just sitting there on her dining room table, and I literally have to walk the long way around through the living room just so I don't have to look at it. I know I need to help him go through it, I'm usually the one who handles all our paperwork and finances because I'm good with spreadsheets and numbers, but I just can't make myself go near it. If I even reach my hand out toward that folder, my chest gets that horrible tight feeling again, and I have to pull away. 

It's even getting to the point where I'm avoiding the most innocent things. Like the herbs on my patio. I had this whole beautiful little container garden out there on our back deck—basil, thyme, rosemary—and I used to spend every weekend morning out there tending to them. But they were right outside the kitchen window, right where the smoke was pouring out. My husband asked if he should bring the surviving pots over to his mom's so I could keep growing them, and I practically yelled at him to just throw them all in the trash. I feel terrible about it, because I loved those plants, but I just cannot be around them. 

It's like anything tied to that day, whether it's the house itself, the paperwork for it, or even just my plants... it all just makes my chest tighten up so badly. I just can’t make myself go near any of it. I know I'm going to have to deal with the mortgage and the house eventually, I can't hide at my mother-in-law's forever, but right now, I just completely avoid it all.

My whole body was just trembling so badly I couldn't even hold a pen. Because that is not how I usually treat people. I am never like that. I'm usually the patient one, the one who helps everyone else keep calm, but lately, it's like this awful switch flips inside me and I just explode. It's exhausting, and I feel terrible about it, but I just can't seem to stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these outbursts happen even when you're not particularly stressed or if they seem way bigger than the situation calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly, that's exactly what I mean. Like I was just saying with that poor girl and the stock on the wrong shelf... my reaction was way, way bigger than the situation called for. I mean, putting a box of items on the middle shelf instead of the bottom shelf is not a crisis, you know? It's literally nothing. \n\nAnd I wasn't even having a bad day before that happened! I had my coffee, the store wasn't even busy yet, it was a Tuesday morning for heaven's sake, which is usually our slowest day of the whole week. So there was absolutely no reason for me to be stressed out. I was just walking the floor, doing my usual morning checks, and then I saw it and... boom. I just completely blew up at her. It was like going from zero to a hundred in a split second over something so trivial. \n\nAnd like I mentioned a minute ago, the aftermath of it... having to hide in the back room and just shaking like a leaf... it just proves to me how out of proportion the whole thing was. Because if I was just normally annoyed about a minor mistake, I wouldn't have a physical reaction like that, you know? I wouldn't be trembling so hard I couldn't even hold my inventory clipboard. It's just so unlike me, it really, really is. I've been managing people in retail for years, and you have to have a lot of patience for this industry. Which I always have had! I'm the calm one. But now, it doesn't matter if it's a slow day or if I'm completely fine one minute, the tiniest little thing sets me off and I'm yelling before I even realize what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any episodes of feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, separate from these anger outbursts?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down or hopeless. I mean, I'm upset about the anger, obviously, because like I said, it's just so unlike me, but it's not like a deep sadness or depression or anything like that. I still look forward to things. Like, my sister and I, we have this tradition where we go to the farmer's market every Sunday morning—rain or shine, we're always there by 8 AM to get the best produce and these amazing scones from this one specific bakery stand—and I still love doing that. I still get excited about it. \n\nAnd I still like my hobbies, too. I do a lot of gardening, mostly container gardening on my patio because I don't have a huge yard, but I've got these gorgeous heirloom tomatoes growing right now, and I still go out there every evening to check on them and water them. I haven't lost interest in any of that. It's just... it's just this anger, this explosive anger that comes out of nowhere. That's the only thing that's really changed. I'm not sitting around crying or feeling like life is pointless, I'm just... I'm just terrified of when the next outburst is going to happen, you know? But as far as feeling hopeless or losing interest in my life, no, that hasn't been happening at all. I still want to do all my normal things, I just want to do them without feeling like a ticking time bomb.\n\nDoctor: Between these outbursts, do you find yourself feeling constantly irritable or on edge, even when nothing is really wrong?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, it's not a constant, simmering kind of thing. I mean, I'm not walking around all day just gritting my teeth or feeling like everyone is annoying me. Between the outbursts, I feel pretty much like my normal self, honestly. I'm just going about my day, doing my job, talking to customers, checking inventory... I'm not on edge in the sense that I'm waiting for a fight. \n\nThat's why it's so shocking when it happens! Like I was saying before, about yelling at that poor girl over the stock on the wrong shelf... I wasn't feeling irritable before that moment. I wasn't pacing around feeling tense. I was perfectly fine. And then, bam, I'm blowing up at people over absolutely nothing. And it really is nothing! And then I'm hiding in the back room shaking because, like I said, that is just not how I usually treat people. It's the suddenness of it, the way it just spikes out of nowhere, that's what's so terrifying. If I was just in a bad mood all day, at least I could warn people, you know? Like, 'Hey, I'm feeling cranky today, give me some space.' But I don't get that warning. I'm fine, I'm fine, I'm fine, and then I'm screaming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually energized, overly confident, or needed less sleep than usual, where your thoughts were racing or you were more easily distracted?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I'm a good sleeper, usually. I need my solid eight hours, or I'm just dragging the next day, especially with being on my feet all day at the store. I've never been one of those people who can just run on four hours of sleep and a pot of coffee. And as for feeling unusually energized or overly confident... definitely not. I mean, I know my job, I'm confident in my abilities as a manager because, like I said, I've been doing this for years, but I don't get these bursts of feeling like I can conquer the world or anything. \n\nMy thoughts don't really race, either. I'm pretty organized, I have to be to keep track of all the inventory and the schedules and the vendor deliveries. If my thoughts were racing and I was getting easily distracted, I wouldn't be able to do my job at all. I have a whole system with my clipboard and my color-coded pens—blue for scheduling, red for inventory issues, green for vendor notes—and I stick to it religiously. I've never had a period where I just couldn't focus or felt like my brain was going a million miles a minute. It's really just... it's just these sudden anger explosions. That's the only thing that's out of the ordinary for me. Everything else, my sleep, my focus, my energy levels, it's all just... normal. Well, except for the shaking after I yell at someone, but that's just because I'm so upset with myself, you know? But no, no racing thoughts or needing less sleep. Definitely not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or been through any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently or in the past that might still be affecting you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's... that's kind of the elephant in the room, I guess. My husband and I, we just bought this older house, a real fixer-upper, about a month ago. We were so excited to finally get out of our apartment and have some space, you know? The plan was to do a lot of the work ourselves on the weekends to save money. We had it all mapped out, room by room.\n\nBut then, during our very first weekend working on the house—this was about two weeks ago, exactly two weeks ago this past Saturday—we were starting on the kitchen. We were just trying to remove some of the old cabinets, and my husband was using this heat gun to soften some old adhesive, and... I don't even know how it happened so fast, but it caught the wall on fire. It was just this tiny spark at first, and then suddenly the whole corner behind the stove was up in flames. \n\nIt was terrifying. The smoke was so thick and black, and it smelled awful, like melting plastic and old grease. I remember screaming at him to get out, and we were running around trying to find the fire extinguisher we'd literally just bought the day before, but in the panic, I couldn't remember where we put it. We ended up having to call 911 and wait outside on the lawn while the fire department came. They put it out quickly, thank God, and the structural damage wasn't too bad, mostly just smoke and water damage in that one room, but... \n\nI mean, ever since then, for the last two weeks, I just haven't been right. That's when all this started. These anger outbursts, the shaking, all of it. It's like a switch flipped the day of that fire, and I haven't been able to turn it back off. Every time I smell anything even remotely like smoke, even just someone grilling outside, my heart starts pounding. But yeah, that fire two weeks ago... that's definitely the only major thing that's happened.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you had any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear, like you were having a panic attack, maybe with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you were losing control?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't get those sudden panic attacks where I feel like I'm dying or losing control or anything. It's not like that. Like I said, the main thing is just the anger, the snapping at people. And the shaking afterwards, but that's just because I feel so awful about yelling. \n\nI mean, yes, if I smell smoke, like I mentioned, my heart will start pounding a little bit, and I get this rush of anxiety, but it's not a full-blown panic attack. It's just a reaction to the smell, you know? It makes sense. It reminds me of the fire. But it's not this out-of-the-blue, overwhelming terror where I can't breathe. I'm not hyperventilating or feeling like the walls are closing in. \n\nI've had friends who have panic attacks, and they describe it as this sudden, terrifying feeling that comes out of nowhere, where they literally think they're having a heart attack and need to go to the hospital. My friend Sarah, she used to get them all the time when we were in our twenties, and she would have to pull her car over on the side of the highway because she couldn't catch her breath. I've never had anything like that. Even when the fire was actually happening, I was panicked, of course, but it was a very specific, logical panic because my house was on fire! I was yelling and running around looking for the extinguisher, but I wasn't frozen in fear or feeling like I was losing my mind. \n\nSo, no, since the fire, I haven't had any of those sudden, intense panic episodes you're talking about. I'm just dealing with the anger outbursts and the lingering anxiety when I smell something burning, but not the racing heart and shortness of breath out of nowhere. I'm pretty grounded, physically, I guess, except for the shaking after I get mad. But that's totally different, right? That's just adrenaline from the yelling, I think. Not a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you found yourself having upsetting thoughts or memories about it that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes. Oh my gosh, yes. It's awful, actually. It's like my brain just won't let it go, no matter how hard I try to focus on other things, and I really do try. It’s not even like I’m sitting there dwelling on it on purpose, you know? It just... the fire keeps jumping into my head completely out of nowhere. \n\nI could be doing the most mundane, everyday things. Like, I'll be at work, just sitting at my desk in the back office, opening up a completely normal inventory spreadsheet on the computer—something I do literally a dozen times a day to check the stock levels—and suddenly, bam, I'm right back there. Or I'll be at home, or well, at my mother-in-law's since we can't really cook in our place right now, and I'll just pick up a frying pan to make eggs or something, and it's like I'm instantly transported back to that Saturday. \n\nIt's so vivid, too. It's not just a passing thought. I can literally smell that awful, toxic smoke all over again, that melting plastic smell I was telling you about earlier. And I can actually see the kitchen just filling up with this thick, black smoke, right in front of my eyes, before I can even stop the memory from happening. It just takes over. \n\nAnd it's so frustrating because I'll be right in the middle of looking at my spreadsheet or talking to one of my cashiers, and I have to grip the edge of the desk or the handle of the pan because the memory is just so intense and real. I try to shake my head and clear it out, I really do, but once it starts, it just forces its way in. It's exhausting, honestly. I just want to look at my spreadsheets and do my job without feeling like my kitchen is burning down all over again. I mean, it's just a pan, you know? But my brain doesn't seem to care. It just flashes right back to the smoke.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you been having nightmares or trouble sleeping that might be related to what happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, sleep? You know, you would think so, right? With everything else going on, those images popping up at work and the anger... you'd think my nights would just be a total disaster. My husband, actually, he was asking me the exact same thing just the other morning. He was like, \"Are you having bad dreams?\" because he knows how stressed I've been since that Saturday. \n\nBut honestly? No. I really haven't had any nightmares about the fire. Not a single one. It’s strange, almost. I mean, I go to bed, usually around 10:30 or 11:00 after we watch a little TV in his mom's living room—we've been watching this baking show just to keep our minds off our own ruined kitchen, ironically enough—and once my head hits the pillow, I am just out cold. \n\nI think I'm just so completely exhausted from dealing with the memories during the day, and all that adrenaline from when I get angry and start shaking... by the time night rolls around, my body just completely shuts down. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in a cold sweat thinking about the smoke. I sleep right through until my alarm goes off at 6:15 for work. It's actually the only time I get any peace, to be perfectly honest with you. During the day, my brain is just going a mile a minute, but at night... nothing. Just blank, heavy sleep. \n\nI guess I should count myself lucky for that, at least, because if I wasn't sleeping on top of everything else, I don't know how I'd be functioning at the store at all. Managing the floor and dealing with customers is hard enough when you're fully rested, you know? So, no, no bad dreams or anything like that. Just... I'm just really tired by the time I get to bed.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities that remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Avoid? Yes. Oh, absolutely. I mean, I’ve been completely avoiding the house itself, for starters. My husband goes over there to deal with the contractors and the insurance adjusters, and he keeps asking if I want to come with him, just to see the progress or help make decisions about the cleanup, but I just... I can't do it. I can't even drive down our street. Just the thought of pulling into that driveway makes my chest tighten up so much, like there’s this heavy, physical weight sitting right on my ribs. \n\nAnd it’s not just the physical house, either. It’s anything even remotely connected to it or that day. Like, the mortgage folder. My husband brought this thick green folder over to his mom's house—it has all our home insurance policies and the mortgage documents in it, because obviously we have to file all these claims and figure out how we're paying for everything. It’s just sitting there on her dining room table, and I literally have to walk the long way around through the living room just so I don't have to look at it. I know I need to help him go through it, I'm usually the one who handles all our paperwork and finances because I'm good with spreadsheets and numbers, but I just can't make myself go near it. If I even reach my hand out toward that folder, my chest gets that horrible tight feeling again, and I have to pull away. \n\nIt's even getting to the point where I'm avoiding the most innocent things. Like the herbs on my patio. I had this whole beautiful little container garden out there on our back deck—basil, thyme, rosemary—and I used to spend every weekend morning out there tending to them. But they were right outside the kitchen window, right where the smoke was pouring out. My husband asked if he should bring the surviving pots over to his mom's so I could keep growing them, and I practically yelled at him to just throw them all in the trash. I feel terrible about it, because I loved those plants, but I just cannot be around them. \n\nIt's like anything tied to that day, whether it's the house itself, the paperwork for it, or even just my plants... it all just makes my chest tighten up so badly. I just can’t make myself go near any of it. I know I'm going to have to deal with the mortgage and the house eventually, I can't hide at my mother-in-law's forever, but right now, I just completely avoid it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Just blank, heavy sleep. \n\nI guess I should count myself lucky for that, at least, because if I wasn't sleeping on top of everything else, I don't know how I'd be functioning at the store at all. Managing the floor and dealing with customers is hard enough when you're fully rested, you know? So, no, no bad dreams or anything like that. Just... I'm just really tired by the time I get to bed.\n\nDoctor: Since the fire, have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities that remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Avoid? Yes. Oh, absolutely. I mean, I’ve been completely avoiding the house itself, for starters. My husband goes over there to deal with the contractors and the insurance adjusters, and he keeps asking if I want to come with him, just to see the progress or help make decisions about the cleanup, but I just... I can't do it. I can't even drive down our street. Just the thought of pulling into that driveway makes my chest tighten up so much, like there’s this heavy, physical weight sitting right on my ribs. \n\nAnd it’s not just the physical house, either. My husband brought this thick green folder over to his mom's house—it has all our home insurance policies and the mortgage documents in it, because obviously we have to file all these claims and figure out how we're paying for everything. It’s just sitting there on her dining room table, and I literally have to walk the long way around through the living room just so I don't have to look at it. I know I need to help him go through it, I'm usually the one who handles all our paperwork and finances because I'm good with spreadsheets and numbers, but I just can't make myself go near it. If I even reach my hand out toward that folder, my chest gets that horrible tight feeling again, and I have to pull away. \n\nIt's even getting to the point where I'm avoiding the most innocent things. Numb? Oh, gosh, no. No, if anything, it’s the exact opposite of that. I feel like my emotions are just dialed up to a hundred all the time right now. I’m definitely not detached from people at all. Like my husband, for instance, we’re talking constantly about all this, and I feel so incredibly guilty that he’s the one having to deal with the contractors and the insurance adjusters while I’m basically hiding out at his mom’s house. And his mom, honestly, she’s been an absolute saint through all of this. Letting us take over her guest room, watching those baking shows with me every night... I feel very connected to them. Maybe even more so than usual, just because I’m leaning on them so heavily right now to get through the days. 

And at work, too. I mean, managing a retail floor, you have to be "on," you know? I’m still talking to my cashiers, still helping customers find things, still doing my rounds. I’m not zoning out or feeling disconnected from them at all. I mean, I might get frustrated more easily, like I was saying earlier about the anger and how my hands start shaking, but I’m definitely present and connected to what's going on around me. 

It’s funny you ask about numbness, actually, because a coworker of mine went through a really bad car accident a few years ago, and she told me she just felt totally hollow for months afterward, just like a zombie. She said she couldn't even cry. I remember thinking how awful that sounded, to just feel nothing. But for me, with this kitchen fire, it’s just... I’m feeling everything. The panic when I see a frying pan, the anger, the guilt over making my husband throw away my patio plants, the stress every time I even look in the direction of that green mortgage folder... it’s all just so loud. There’s no numbness at all. 

Honestly, I almost wish there was a little bit of numbness, just to give me a break for five minutes, you know? Because feeling every single thing so intensely, especially when I’m just trying to check my inventory spreadsheets or do normal things, it’s just completely exhausting. But no, to answer your question, I definitely still feel connected to my family and everyone else. I'm just a giant ball of stress while I'm doing it.

It's exhausting, and I feel terrible about it, but I just can't seem to stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if these outbursts happen even when you're not particularly stressed or if they seem way bigger than the situation calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly, that's exactly what I mean. I feel terrible about it, because I loved those plants, but I just cannot be around them. \n\nIt's like anything tied to that day, whether it's the house itself, the paperwork for it, or even just my plants... it all just makes my chest tighten up so badly. I just can’t make myself go near any of it. Oh, gosh, no. No, if anything, it’s the exact opposite of that. I feel like my emotions are just dialed up to a hundred all the time right now. I’m definitely not detached from people at all. Like my husband, for instance, we’re talking constantly about all this, and I feel so incredibly guilty that he’s the one having to deal with the contractors and the insurance adjusters while I’m basically hiding out at his mom’s house. And his mom, honestly, she’s been an absolute saint through all of this. Letting us take over her guest room, watching those baking shows with me every night... I feel very connected to them. Maybe even more so than usual, just because I’m leaning on them so heavily right now to get through the days. \n\nAnd at work, too. I mean, managing a retail floor, you have to be \"on,\" you know? I’m still talking to my cashiers, still helping customers find things, still doing my rounds. I’m not zoning out or feeling disconnected from them at all. She said she couldn't even cry. I remember thinking how awful that sounded, to just feel nothing. But for me, with this kitchen fire, it’s just... I’m feeling everything. The panic when I see a frying pan, the anger, the guilt over making my husband throw away my patio plants, the stress every time I even look in the direction of that green mortgage folder... it’s all just so loud. There’s no numbness at all. \n\nHonestly, I almost wish there was a little bit of numbness, just to give me a break for five minutes, you know? Because feeling every single thing so intensely, especially when I’m just trying to check my inventory spreadsheets or do normal things, it’s just completely exhausting. But no, to answer your question, I definitely still feel connected to my family and everyone else. I'm just a giant ball of stress while I'm doing it."
